Ladder could be another exit from storeroom? Ladder maybe leads elsewhere. That contradicts "only exit". Spatial reasoning: ladder is a passage, so there is another exit.

Thus evidence 17 (Ms. Hart's Picture) contradicts testimony 3.

Check rule: If X passes through Y and Z is inside Y and Z overlaps X then Z is pierced. Not relevant.

If X falls onto Y and Y is covered by Z then X is covered by Z. Not.

If X contains Y and Y covers Z then X contains Z. Not.

If X covers Y then Y is covered by X. Not.

Thus no rule applies. Use spatial reasoning: picture shows ladder leading upwards, indicating another exit besides hatch.

Thus answer: evidence 17, testimony 3.

**Step 3 – How the rule reveals the contradiction**  
Because the victim cannot fall onto an umbrella that is “in a distance” away, the statement that he fell on the umbrella conflicts with the visual evidence.

**Step 4 – Spatial reasoning without a rule**  
By comparing the positions depicted in the photograph (umbrella far from the victim) with the testimony (victim on the umbrella), we see a clear mismatch in the described spatial arrangement.

**Conclusion** – The contradictory pair is Evidence 2 and Testimony 3.

Not relevant.

But we can still note spatial reasoning: orientation.

Thus answer: evidence 6, testimony 3.

**Step 1:**  
Evidence 6 (Witness’s Photo) shows the victim standing on the bridge, facing left, with a man in a jail uniform standing to her left and facing her. This places the man **in front of** the victim.  
Testimony 3 (Gumshoe) states that “the criminal **shoved the victim down from behind** and stabbed her in the back,” which requires the attacker to be **behind** the victim at the moment of the shove.

**Step 2:**  
The spatial rule *“If XXX is hit from behind then XXX falls forward”* applies to the situation described in Testimony 3.

**Step 3:**  
Applying the rule: if the victim were indeed hit from behind, she would fall **forward** (toward the attacker). However, the photo (Evidence 6) shows the attacker positioned **in front** of the victim, not behind her. Therefore the spatial configuration in the evidence contradicts the description in the testimony.

**Step 4:**  
Even without the rule, the simple spatial relationship—attacker in front vs. attacker behind—creates a direct inconsistency between what is visually documented (Evidence 6) and what is verbally claimed (Testimony 3).

**Conclusion:** The contradiction is between Evidence 6 and Testimony 3.

**Step 3:** - Rule used: *If XXX is in the same room as YYY then XXX can potentially see YYY.*  
  - Because Lotta is not in the same “room” (location) as the lake, she cannot see the lake directly; the camera’s automatic trigger should have recorded the event without her needing binoculars. Her statement that she looked with binoculars contradicts the camera’s automatic function.

**Step 4:** Even without the explicit rule, the contradiction is evident from the spatial relationship: the camera is set to react to the noise at the lake, while Lotta claims she needed to manually observe the lake after hearing the noise, which would be unnecessary if the camera performed that function.

The chip photo shows near side 4 small +1 large = 1400, far side 2 small +9 large = 9200. Not matching 3500 each. So contradiction.

**Step 1:**  
Testimony 1 states: *“The victim was hit with it from behind, and he fell over, right into the lantern.”*  
Evidence 6 (Photo of Victim) shows the victim *lying face‑up* inside the broken lantern.

**Step 2:**  
None of the listed spatial rules directly address “hit from behind → fall direction,” but the rule *“If XXX is hit from the front then XXX falls backward”* implies a relationship between hit direction and fall orientation. By symmetry, being hit from behind would cause the victim to fall forward, which would normally place the victim face‑down on the ground (or inside the lantern).

**Step 3:**  
Applying the implied opposite of the rule:  
- Hit from behind → fall forward → victim should be face‑down.  
- Evidence 6 shows the victim face‑up, contradicting this expected orientation.

**Step 4:**  
Thus, the spatial inconsistency between the expected posture after a backward hit and the observed face‑up posture in the photograph reveals the contradiction.

**Step 1:**  
Testimony 2 (by Lang) states that the victim “was shot from on high,” implying the shooter was positioned above the victim and the bullet traveled downward.  
Evidence 6 (Mr. Deacon’s Murder Notes) describes the wound: entry at the lower right abdomen and exit at the right shoulder, indicating the bullet traveled from low to high (upward).

**Step 2:**  
The spatial rule “If XXX is shot upward then the trajectory goes from low to high” applies to Evidence 6.

**Step 3:**  
Applying the rule, Evidence 6 tells us the shot was upward (low → high). This directly conflicts with Testimony 2, which claims the shot came from above (high → low). The opposite directions reveal a clear contradiction.

**Step 4:**  
Even without the rule, the physical description of wound locations (abdomen lower than shoulder) contradicts the claim that the shooter was positioned high above the victim.

**Step 1:**  
Testimony 5 (by Andrews) states that she “knocked the flower vase over, where it fell onto the guitar case.”  
Evidence 9 (the crime photo) shows the scene: “Next to the guitar case are the remains of a smashed vase that has stained the carpet with water.” The vase is described as being *next to* the case, not *on* it.

**Step 2:**  
No listed spatial‑reasoning rule directly mentions “next to” versus “on,” but the contradiction is purely spatial: the relative positions of the vase and the guitar case differ between the testimony and the photographic evidence.

**Step 3:**  
Since none of the provided rules apply, we rely on ordinary spatial reasoning: an object that “fell onto” another would be in contact with it (e.g., resting on or partially covering it). The photo shows the vase’s remains merely adjacent to the case, indicating it did **not** fall onto the case.

**Step 4:**  
Thus the testimony’s claim about the vase’s location contradicts the visual evidence of the vase’s actual placement.
